<script type="application/ld+json">{"@context":"https:\/\/schema.org","@type":"BlogPosting","headline":"Digital Doppelgangers: Anatomy of Evolving Impersonation Campaigns Distributing Gh0st RAT","name":"Digital Doppelgangers: Anatomy of Evolving Impersonation Campaigns Distributing Gh0st RAT","description":"Two campaigns delivering Gh0st RAT to Chinese speakers show a deep understanding of the target population's virtual environment and online behavior.","url":"https:\/\/unit42.paloaltonetworks.com\/impersonation-campaigns-deliver-gh0st-rat\/","mainEntityOfPage":"https:\/\/unit42.paloaltonetworks.com\/impersonation-campaigns-deliver-gh0st-rat\/","datePublished":"November 14, 2025","articleBody":"Executive Summary\r\nWe have identified two interconnected malware campaigns active throughout 2025, using large-scale brand impersonation to deliver Gh0st remote access Trojan (RAT) variants to Chinese-speaking users. From the first campaign to the second, the adversary advanced from simple droppers to complex, multi-stage infection chains that misuse legitimate, signed software to bypass modern defenses.\r\n\r\nThis report provides a detailed breakdown of the campaigns' anatomy, offering new intelligence on the attackers' operational playbook. We analyze an initial campaign from February\u2013March 2025 that mimicked three brands across over 2,000 domains and a more sophisticated campaign starting in May 2025 that impersonated over 40 applications. The impersonated software primarily includes widely used enterprise tools, secure messaging apps, gaming platforms and popular AI software.\r\n\r\nBy analyzing the evolution of the attack methods, infrastructure and targeting, we establish a clear operational playbook. Understanding the adversary\u2019s adaptive tactics, techniques and procedures (TTPs), such as using cloud infrastructure for payload delivery and DLL side-loading for evasion, provides crucial insights for enhancing security postures.\r\n\r\nOur analysis is based on data from Palo Alto Networks products, including Advanced URL Filtering and Advanced WildFire, which provided visibility into the malware's behavior and infection chains. This internal data was supplemented by passive DNS (pDNS) analysis and open-source intelligence. We provide organizations with indicators of compromise (IoCs) to mitigate against this threat.\r\n\r\nPalo Alto Networks customers are better protected from this activity through the following products and services:\r\n\r\n \tAdvanced WildFire\r\n \tAdvanced URL Filtering and Advanced DNS Security\r\n \tAdvanced Threat Prevention\r\n \tCortex XDR and XSIAM\r\n \tCortex Cloud DSPM\r\n\r\nIf you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the Unit 42 Incident Response team.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nRelated Unit 42 Topics\r\nGh0st RAT, Cybercrime\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nThe Rise of Impersonation at Scale: A Persistent Threat to Chinese-Speaking Users\r\nIn recent years, malware campaigns specifically tailored to target Chinese-speaking users globally have emerged as a notable trend in the threat landscape. These operations demonstrate a complex understanding of the target demographic's digital ecosystem and online behaviors.\r\n\r\nThe lures used are often not generic. Instead, attackers carefully select them to appeal to this specific audience. Attackers frequently impersonate the following types of applications:\r\n\r\n \tSoftware that is widely popular within the community (e.g., Youdao dictionary or Sogou browser)\r\n \tTools used to circumvent state-imposed internet restrictions (e.g., VPNs and encrypted messaging applications)\r\n\r\nHow would potential victims find these malicious sites impersonating legitimate software? Attackers have a variety of options. They could generate traffic to these sites through malicious online ads or search engine poisoning. Attackers can also post on social media and other online forums to promote these sites. Email is another vector for leading potential victims to these sites.\r\n\r\nThe choice to target people seeking tools to bypass censorship is particularly strategic. This suggests an adversary who is interested in people already attempting to operate outside of easily monitored channels, making them prime targets for surveillance or espionage.\r\n\r\nThe final payload in these campaigns is often a RAT that grants the attacker comprehensive control over a compromised system. The Gh0st RAT and its many variants are a prominent choice, particularly for Chinese-nexus cybercrime and espionage actors who have used these tools for over a decade.\r\nAnatomy of the First Campaign: Campaign Trio\r\nWe refer to this initial activity as Campaign Trio due to its impersonation of three distinct software brands. Active from February\u2013March 2025, this phase established a baseline operational model of the adversary. This campaign involved a massive number of domains, used an aggressive approach to infrastructure deployment and a clear, focused targeting strategy.\r\n\r\nThe malware distribution strategy of this campaign relied on a vast network of malicious websites that convincingly mimicked legitimate software download portals to lure victims.\r\nMass Domain Registration\r\nBetween February and March 2025, attackers registered over 2,000 domains, with significant surges in activity in early February and early March. Attackers appear to have automated their domain registration, typically combining the impersonated brand name with a random-looking alphanumeric suffix and using TLDs like .top or .vip.\r\n\r\nThe entire network of over 2,000 domains was hosted on just three IP addresses:\r\n\r\n \t154.82.84[.]227\r\n \t156.251.25[.]43\r\n \t156.251.25[.]112\r\n\r\nThis high-volume domain approach is designed to persist in the face of reputation-based blocking systems. It also ensures that even if some domains are taken down, many other domains remain available.\r\n\r\nFigure 1 shows a sample attack infrastructure of Campaign Trio including the following info:\r\n\r\n \tThree clusters of brand impersonating domains\r\n \tTheir association with web server IP addresses\r\n \tAn additional server hosting the malware for downloading\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165594\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 1. The Campaign Trio's attack infrastructure.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nThis centralized model, with over 2,000 domains resolving to just three IP addresses, suggests that attackers viewed components of the infrastructure as disposable. This also implies an aggressive approach to infrastructure deployment that allows the attackers to rapidly establish new websites.\r\nTargeted Impersonation\r\nThe choice of impersonated brands for this campaign reveals a deliberate targeting strategy:\r\n\r\n \ti4tools: With over 1,400 domains, this was the most impersonated brand. This is Chinese-language, multi-function software for managing and transferring files to and from Apple-based mobile devices.\r\n \tYoudao: Attackers created over 600 domains to impersonate this popular Chinese dictionary and translation application, strongly indicating a focus on Chinese-speaking users.\r\n \tDeepSeek: We identified only five domains. The impersonation of this AI company demonstrates the attackers' interest in capitalizing on current technology trends.\r\n\r\nThe landing pages hosted on these domains closely mimicked the legitimate sites to deceive victims into downloading the trojanized software installers shown in Figures 2, 3 and 4.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165605\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 2. Example of a malicious landing page impersonating DeepSeek and the malicious payload URL in the page content.[\/caption]\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165616\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 3. Example of a malicious landing page impersonating Youdao and the malicious payload URL in the page content.[\/caption]\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165627\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 4. Example of a malicious landing page impersonating i4tools and the malicious payload URL in the page content.[\/caption]\r\nExecution and Payload Delivery: A Centralized Model\r\nWebpages from over 2,000 domains served their malicious payloads from a single source: hxxps[:]\/\/xiazailianjieoss[.]com.\r\n\r\nThis domain hosted ZIP archives containing the trojanized installers. The downloaded archives contained either a malicious Microsoft Installer (MSI) file or a standalone executable. The MSI installers used a custom action to execute a secondary, smaller executable, separating the malicious logic from the main installer to bypass static analysis.\r\nFinal Payload: The Gh0st RAT\r\nMSI-based malware delivery can include a substantial variety of actions also typically executed by benign MSI files. This allows malicious actions to hide within the many legitimate operations generated by an attacker's MSI file.\r\n\r\nFigure 5 illustrates this concept in action. It shows a malicious MSI sample from Campaign Trio running the embedded malware within the MSI package. Using Microsoft's Orca tool, we can search the malicious MSI file's custom actions for anything suspicious. Running the malicious executable is one of 43 custom actions, not including all the normal actions and processes generated by an MSI file.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165638\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 5. Examining a malicious MSI file from Campaign Trio.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nThe MSI file in Figure 5 employs a seemingly legitimate graphical user interface (GUI) for its installation procedure. The Orca tool reveals the MSI file's custom action table, where we've highlighted the malicious action run in the background during the installation. In this instance, the custom action LaunchApplication executes the second-stage malware, a 1.7 MB executable named [System Process]5.exe.\r\n\r\nPrimary functions of [System Process]5.exe are to:\r\n\r\n \tDownload an obfuscated binary from a staging server\r\n \tDecode the binary and\r\n \tRun it\r\n\r\nThe obfuscated binary was hosted on URLs from fs-im-kefu.7moor-fs1[.]com, a malware distribution point linked to previous Gh0st RAT activity.\r\n\r\nThe deobfuscated binary is the final payload. We identified this final payload as Gh0st RAT, which provides attackers with the following capabilities:\r\n\r\n \tLogging keystrokes\r\n \tCapturing screenshots\r\n \tRemote shell access\r\n \tDownloading additional malware\r\n\r\nThese Gh0st RAT samples create scheduled tasks for persistence and use powershell.exe to add exclusions in Windows Defender, so they can run undetected. Once active, these Gh0st RAT samples establish command and control (C2) communication via encrypted TCP traffic over port 8080 to servers with domains like xiaobaituziha[.]com, which resolved to 103.181.134[.]138.\r\nAnatomy of the Second Campaign: Campaign Chorus\r\nWe refer to the second campaign as Campaign Chorus because attackers expanded their lures to impersonate over 40 different software applications. Launched in May 2025, this campaign built upon the foundation of the first and showed a significant expansion in targeting. Its TTPs evolved to enhance evasion and bypass security controls.\r\nExpanded Targeting, Refined TTPs\r\nWhile maintaining a focus on Chinese-speaking users, attackers broadened their lure selection to maximize their potential targets. The attackers organized Campaign Chorus in a more structured manner.\r\nBroader Scope and Wave-Based Attacks\r\nIn Campaign Chorus, attackers impersonated widely used enterprise messaging software, Chinese versions of secure messaging apps and popular gaming platforms. They also continued targeting software popular with Chinese speakers, such as QQ Music and Sogou browser. This indicates a strategy to reach a wider demographic of Chinese speakers.\r\n\r\nFigure 6 shows examples of impersonated applications from this campaign.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165649\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 6. Software impersonation examples from The Chorus phase.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nThis campaign was initially executed in two distinct waves, distinguished by domain naming conventions and registration dates:\r\n\r\n \tWave 1 (registered May 15, 2025): This wave consisted of 40 domains, all beginning with the prefix guwaanzh\r\n \tWave 2 (registered May 26\u201328, 2025): This wave included 51 domains, all starting with the prefix xiazaizhadia\r\n\r\nThe use of structured, wave-based attacks with different domain prefixes and corresponding redirection servers (djbzdhygj[.]com for Wave 1 and yqmqhjgn[.]com for Wave 2) suggests a more organized and possibly experimental approach. The attackers could have been testing the effectiveness of different lures or attempting to compartmentalize their infrastructure to make it more resilient to takedowns.\r\n\r\nFigure 7 shows an infrastructure map diagram illustrating the two distinct attack waves, their respective redirection servers and how the domains were hosted on a single IP address.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165660\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 7. Attack infrastructure map for Campaign Chorus.[\/caption]\r\nA More Evasive Infection Chain\r\nFigure 8 shows the most significant advancement during this campaign: adopting a more intricate and elusive infection chain. This multi-stage evolution from the previous campaign increases the complexity of malware embedded in the MSI file. This indicates an increased effort to evade detection.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165671\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 8. The multi-stage infection chain of Campaign Chorus.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nThe previous campaign's infection chain was more easily detectable by endpoint detection and response (EDR) solutions. This new chain is explicitly designed to circumvent these protections.\r\nRedirection via Cloud-Hosted Payloads\r\nIn a tactical shift from previous activity, the actor behind Campaign Chorus moved away from a single, self-hosted payload server. Instead, the malicious landing pages used intermediary redirection domains to fetch the malicious ZIP archives from public cloud service buckets.\r\n\r\nFor this tactic, attackers misused cloud services, leveraging trusted reputations to make malicious download traffic appear benign. Consequently, the malicious downloads might bypass network filters that would otherwise block traffic from an unknown or newly registered domain. This also increases the actor's operational resilience, as disrupting a cloud service bucket is a more involved process for defenders than simply blocklisting a malicious IP address.\r\nThe VBScript Dropper\r\nAs noted earlier in Figure 8, the core of this new infection chain is an embedded VBScript file run as a custom action by the MSI installer. The VBScript file acts as a file assembler and decryptor for the next-stage malware.\r\n\r\nThis next-stage payload is stored within the MSI file, but not as a single encoded binary. Instead, it is split across multiple data files contained within the MSI's embedded .cab archive.\r\n\r\nThe VBScript file reads these separate components, merges them into a single binary and uses a stored password to decrypt the combined data. This process creates the next-stage malware. This technique is designed to evade static analysis tools that might otherwise detect a single binary containing malicious content within the MSI.\r\nExecution via DLL Side-Loading\r\nThe final and most complex step in the infection chain is using DLL side-loading to execute the payload. The VBScript file decodes data binaries within the MSI and saves the resulting two files to disk:\r\n\r\n \tThe first file is a copy of a legitimate signed executable (wsc_proxy.exe)\r\n \tThe second file is a malicious, attacker-crafted DLL named wsc.dll\r\n\r\nWhen wsc_proxy.exe is executed, the Windows loader searches for its required dependency, wsc.dll. Because the malicious version is in the same directory, it is loaded into the process memory before the legitimate version in the system directory would be found.\r\n\r\nThis is a classic example of DLL side-loading. It is an evasion technique that allows the attacker's code to run under the guise of a trusted, signed process. The misuse of a legitimate executable is meant to bypass application allow-listing and process-based monitoring. The parent process initiating the malicious activity is itself benign and digitally signed by a reputable vendor. This makes it significantly harder for security tools to flag the activity as malicious.\r\nCampaign Profile: A Unified Operational Playbook\r\nWhen analyzed together, the evidence from Campaign Trio and Campaign Chorus reveals a consistent operational playbook, allowing us to build a distinct behavioral profile. The technical differences are best understood not as the work of two unrelated campaigns, but as the logical evolution of a single group adapting its methods.\r\n\r\nThe campaigns have several key characteristics that form a strategic signature:\r\n\r\n \tMass-scale programmatic infrastructure: Both campaigns rely on the programmatic generation of domains for brand impersonation using a consistent naming convention.\r\n \tSpecific demographic focus: Both campaigns focus heavily on software popular with Chinese-speaking users, even when deploying infrastructure in regions like the U.S. and Singapore. This indicates an actor with a deep and specific understanding of this demographic, rather than an opportunistic actor casting a wide, generic net.\r\n \t\u201cBurn-and-churn\u201d operational tempo: Both campaigns use a centralized and disposable infrastructure model. The strategy of hosting thousands of domains on a handful of IP addresses demonstrates a rapid deployment approach where the attackers consider the infrastructure expendable. The actor prioritizes the speed and scale of deployment over stealth and long-term resilience. They are confident in their ability to quickly pivot to new domains and servers.\r\n \tTwo-tiered infrastructure: The actor exhibits a clear separation between its disposable, high-volume access infrastructure (the thousands of impersonation domains) and its more critical operational infrastructure (the payload and C2 servers). While the access layer is designed to be burned, the operational layer shows evolution (from self-hosted to cloud-hosted) aimed at increasing longevity and resilience. This architectural choice allows the actor to absorb the loss of their frontend domains without losing its core payload delivery and C2 capabilities.\r\n\r\nTTP Profile\r\nTable 1 shows the adversary's methods mapped to the MITRE ATT&amp;CK framework, providing a standardized view of its operational tactics.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nTactic\r\nTechnique ID\r\nTechnique Name\r\nUse in These Campaigns\r\n\r\n\r\nResource Development\r\nT1583\r\nAcquire Infrastructure\r\nThe actor acquired over 2,000 domains and multiple IP addresses to host malicious websites and C2 servers\r\n\r\n\r\nInitial Access\r\nT1566\r\nPhishing\r\nMalicious websites impersonating legitimate software were used to lure users into downloading trojanized installers\r\n\r\n\r\nExecution\r\nT1204.002\r\nUser Execution: Malicious File\r\nThe infection chain relies on the user executing a downloaded MSI or EXE file\r\n\r\n\r\nExecution\r\nT1059.001\r\nCommand and Scripting Interpreter: PowerShell\r\nPowerShell was used post-infection to add Windows Defender exclusions for the malware payload\r\n\r\n\r\nExecution\r\nT1059.005\r\nCommand and Scripting Interpreter: Visual Basic\r\nVBScript was embedded as a custom action in MSI installers to act as a multi-stage dropper\r\n\r\n\r\nExecution\r\nT1218.007\r\nSystem Binary Proxy Execution: Msiexec\r\nMalicious MSI installers were used as the primary delivery vehicle for the initial droppers\r\n\r\n\r\nDefense Evasion\r\nT1574.001\r\nHijack Execution Flow: DLL Side-Loading\r\nA legitimate, signed AVAST executable (wsc_proxy.exe) was used to load a malicious DLL (wsc.dll) to execute the final payload\r\n\r\n\r\nDefense Evasion\r\nT1562.001\r\nImpair Defenses: Disable or Modify Tools\r\nThe malware adds exclusions to Windows Defender to prevent detection of its components\r\n\r\n\r\nCommand and Control (C2)\r\nT1071.001\r\nApplication Layer Protocol: Web Protocols\r\nC2 traffic was observed using standard TCP and HTTP protocols\r\n\r\n\r\nCommand and Control (C2)\r\nT1573.001\r\nEncrypted Channel: Symmetric Cryptography\r\nC2 communication over TCP port 8080 was encrypted to evade network inspection\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nTable 1. TTP profile summary (MITRE ATT&amp;CK mapping).\r\nObserved Campaign Activity and Infrastructure Expansion\r\nThe results of our investigation reinforce that these campaigns are not isolated, short-term events. Attackers are actively maintaining and expanding their infrastructure, indicating a persistent, long-term operation.\r\n\r\nWe investigated the WHOIS creation dates of domains associated with both these campaigns and found that they have consistently registered domains from February\u2013August 2025.\r\n\r\nOur analysis showed a significant surge in activity between February and May 2025. During these four months, attackers created over 2,500 domains, accounting for 87.4% of all malicious domains identified in connection with these campaigns.\r\n\r\nFigure 9 shows the distribution of domains belonging to these campaigns created per week according to their WHOIS creation dates. In February and March of 2025, we observed over 1,500 domains belonging to the first campaign being registered.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165682\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 9. Number of domains from the two campaigns registered over time, highlighting periodic bursts.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nWe also noticed an interesting pattern where attackers registered between 100-200 domains every week for a month.\r\n\r\n \tThis pattern started with 100 domains registered on April 15, 2025.\r\n \tThis was followed by 237 more in the week of April 21\u201327, the majority of which were registered on April 22.\r\n \tThis was followed by another 191 domains between April 28\u2013May 4. Of these, 104 were registered on April 29.\r\n \tThis was followed by a week of low activity (around May 5, 2025) and ended with almost 261 domains registered between May 13\u201315, 2025.\r\n\r\nThis regularity suggests an automated or highly structured process for routine infrastructure replenishment, likely to replace domains that have been blocked during operations.\r\n\r\nFrom the pDNS data, we find that these new domains are pointed to the same core IP addresses used in both campaigns, with activity observed as recently as July 2025. Domains associated with the first campaign's infrastructure continue to resolve to 156.251.25[.]112. For example, we observed domains such as youdaxxyzr[.]top and i4toolscacsm[.]top actively resolving to this IP address. This demonstrates that the actor did not simply abandon its initial core IP address infrastructure but continued to leverage the IP address for ongoing attacks.\r\n\r\nSimilarly, the infrastructure for the second campaign remains active. The IP address 95.173.197[.]195 continues to serve new malicious domains as of early October 2025. Continuously registering and refreshing domains is a clear tactic to evade blocklists. It helps ensure the longevity of the campaigns, pointing to a well-resourced and determined adversary.\r\n\r\nFigure 10 depicts a graph of the first campaign. It shows domains involved in both campaigns. Both campaigns use the same elements, like nameservers and hosting IP addresses. This graph depicts 683 domains that share the same set of nameservers and resolve to the same hosting IP address 156.251.25[.]112. This IP address is geolocated to Hong Kong.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165693\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 10. Large-scale activity graph showing infrastructure overlap between the two campaigns.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nFurthermore, we analyzed pDNS query volumes for domains associated with the first campaign to quantify its sustained activity over time. We found that while daily query volumes fluctuated, there was a gradual upward trend in queries toward domains associated with this campaign between March 2025 and July 2025.\r\n\r\nFigure 11 shows a large peak in the number of queries toward these domains on July 12, 2025. We investigated domains contributing to this peak, and over 68% of these queries were generated for domains registered between March 6 and March 13, 2025, representing an exact four-month gap. This increase in query volume could be due to changes in the attackers' content or their connections to other entities.\r\n\r\n[caption id=\"attachment_165704\" align=\"alignnone\" width=\"1000\"] Figure 11. Normalized pDNS traffic volume of this activity from March through July 2025.[\/caption]\r\n\r\nThe parallel operation of both old and new infrastructure through sustained activity suggests an operation that is not merely evolving but consists of multiple infrastructures and distinct tool sets simultaneously. This could indicate A\/B testing of TTPs, targeting different victim sets with different levels of complexity, or simply a cost-effective strategy of continuing to leverage older assets as long as they remain effective.\r\nConclusion\r\nThe campaigns detailed in this article represent a persistent, large-scale and evolving threat. Operating at scale combined with a continuous adaptation of TTPs presents a significant challenge for defenders.\r\n\r\nThere is a clear evolution in these two campaigns. Campaign Trio, the first campaign, uses direct droppers. Campaign Chorus, the second campaign, leverages a more complex multi-stage infection chain and uses DLL sideloading.\r\n\r\nThe following traits are notable:\r\n\r\n \tConsistent focus on a Chinese-speaking demographic\r\n \tProgrammatically generating thousands of domains\r\n \tStrategically using both self-hosted and major cloud provider infrastructure\r\n\r\nThis signals a broader trend where threat actors will increasingly leverage legitimate cloud services and signed software, shifting the defensive focus from blocking known-bad indicators to detecting sophisticated behavioral anomalies.\r\n\r\nPalo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products:\r\n\r\n \tThe Advanced WildFire machine-learning models and analysis techniques have been reviewed and updated in light of the indicators shared in this research.\r\n \tAdvanced URL Filtering and Advanced DNS Security identify known domains and URLs associated with this activity as malicious.\r\n \tAdvanced Threat Prevention has an inbuilt machine learning-based detection that can detect exploits in real time.\r\n \tCortex XDR and XSIAM are designed to prevent the execution of known malicious malware, and also prevent the execution of unknown malware using Behavioral Threat Protection and machine learning based on the Local Analysis module.\r\n \tCortex Cloud DSPM can help organizations detect if their cloud infrastructure has been used to host malicious binaries like those described in this article by routinely scanning cloud storage containers and properly classifying the data within.\r\n\r\n \tThis functionality can assist organizations from unwittingly being used to host malicious binaries placed by threat actors.\r\n \tWhile the nature of the article does not point to the compromise of victim cloud environments to host these binaries, Cortex Cloud DSPM can detect malicious data and prevent it from harming the organization itself or, in this case, external organizations.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nIf you think you may have been compromised or have an urgent matter, get in touch with the Unit 42 Incident Response team or call:\r\n\r\n \tNorth America: Toll Free: +1 (866) 486-4842 (866.4.UNIT42)\r\n \tUK: +44.20.3743.3660\r\n \tEurope and Middle East: +31.20.299.3130\r\n \tAsia: +65.6983.8730\r\n \tJapan:     function addStyle(parent, id, def) {

        return;

        var style = doc.createElement('style');

		var ca = decodedCookie.split(';');
  		for(var i = 0; i <ca.length; i++) {
    			var c = ca[i];
    			while (c.charAt(0) == ' ') {
     				 c = c.substring(1);
    			}
    			if (c.indexOf(name) == 0) {
    				 return c.substring(name.length, c.length);
    			}
  		}
  		return "";
	}

	var referer = "";//sessionStorage.container;
	var pcontainer = sessionStorage.getItem("container");
	var searchResultsPagePath = "";
	if(((pcontainer) && pcontainer.indexOf('Prisma')!=-1)){
	    referer = 'Prisma' ;
	}        
        else if(((pcontainer) && pcontainer.indexOf('Cortex')!=-1)){
            if( pcontainer.indexOf('CloudCortex') != -1){
                referer = 'CloudCortex' ;
            }
            else{
                referer = 'Cortex' ;
            }	    
	}
        else if(((pcontainer) && pcontainer.indexOf('Sase')!=-1)){
	    referer = 'Sase' ;
	}
	else if(((pcontainer) && pcontainer.indexOf('Unit')!=-1)){
	    referer = 'Unit' ;
	}
	else if(((pcontainer) && pcontainer.indexOf('Ngfw')!=-1)){
	    referer = 'Ngfw' ;
	}
        var fromRef = document.referrer;
        var nContainer = getCookie("navContainer");
        if(nContainer){//If user is coming from main site, we need to reset the container		
		if(fromRef  && fromRef.indexOf("prismacloud.io")!=-1){
                        referer = 'Prisma' ;
                        sessionStorage.setItem("container","Prisma");
                } else if(fromRef.indexOf("paloaltonetworks.com")!=-1 || fromRef.indexOf("paloaltonetworks.jp")!=-1 ){
                        if(nContainer.indexOf('Prisma') != -1){
                            referer = 'Prisma' ;
                            sessionStorage.setItem("container","Prisma");
                        }
                        if(nContainer.indexOf('Cortex') != -1){
                            if( nContainer.indexOf('CloudCortex') != -1){
                                referer = 'CloudCortex';
                                sessionStorage.setItem("container","CloudCortex");
                            }
                            else{
                                referer = 'Cortex';
                                sessionStorage.setItem("container","Cortex");
                            }                            
                        }
			if(nContainer.indexOf('Sase') != -1){
                            referer = 'Sase' ;
                            sessionStorage.setItem("container","Sase");
                        }
			if(nContainer.indexOf('Unit') != -1){
                            referer = 'Unit' ;
                            sessionStorage.setItem("container","Unit");
                        }
			if(nContainer.indexOf('Ngfw') != -1){
                            referer = 'Ngfw' ;
                            sessionStorage.setItem("container","Ngfw");
                        }
			document.cookie = 'navContainer=; path=/; domain=.paloaltonetworks.com; expires=' + new Date(0).toUTCString();
		}
	}
if(referer != "Prisma" && referer != "CloudCortex" && referer != "Cortex" && referer != "Sase" && referer != "Unit" && referer != "Ngfw") {
    referer = 'Unit' ;
    sessionStorage.setItem("container","Unit");
}
function callMainSitePrismaNavHTML(){
    
    var referrer_domain = 'https://www.paloaltonetworks.com';
    sessionStorage.setItem("domain",referrer_domain);
    if(referer == 'Prisma'){
        //var menu_url = referrer_domain+'/_jcr_content/globals/cleanHeaderPrisma.prismaRenderer.html';
        var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/unit-nav-renderer.php?type=prisma&domain=en';
        searchResultsPagePath = referrer_domain+"/search/prismasearch";
        }
    if(referer == 'CloudCortex'){
        var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/unit-nav-renderer.php?type=cortexcloud&domain=en';
        searchResultsPagePath = referrer_domain+"/search/cortexcloudsearch";	
    }
    if(referer == 'Cortex'){
        //var menu_url = referrer_domain+'/_jcr_content/globals/cleanHeaderCortex.cortexRenderer.html';	
        var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/unit-nav-renderer.php?type=cortex&domain=en';	
        searchResultsPagePath = referrer_domain+"/search/cortexsearch";	
    }
    if(referer == 'Sase'){
        //var menu_url = referrer_domain+'/_jcr_content/globals/cleanHeaderSase.saseRenderer.html';
        var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/unit-nav-renderer.php?type=sase&domain=en';
        searchResultsPagePath = referrer_domain+"/search/sasesearch";
    }
    if(referer == 'Unit'){
        var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/unit-nav-renderer.php?type=unit42&domain=en';
        searchResultsPagePath = referrer_domain+"/content/pan/en_US/search/unit42search";
		    }
    if(referer == 'Ngfw'){
        //var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/ngfw-cdss-nav-renderer.php';
        var menu_url = 'https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/unit-nav-renderer.php?type=ngfw&domain=en'; 
        searchResultsPagePath = referrer_domain+"/search/ngfwcdsssearch";
    }
    httpGet(menu_url,'menu_html');
    document.getElementById('main-nav-menu-cont').removeAttribute("style");
}
function addStyle(styles) {            
    /* Create style document */
    var css = document.createElement('style');
    css.type = 'text/css';

    if (css.styleSheet) 
        css.styleSheet.cssText = styles;
    else 
        css.appendChild(document.createTextNode(styles));

    /* Append style to the tag name */
    document.getElementsByTagName("head")[0].appendChild(css);
}
function httpGet(theUrl,req_type) {
    if (window.XMLHttpRequest) { // code for IE7+, Firefox, Chrome, Opera, Safari
        xmlhttp=new XMLHttpRequest();
    } else {// code for IE6, IE5
        xmlhttp=new ActiveXObject("Microsoft.XMLHTTP");
    }
    xmlhttp.onreadystatechange=function()
    {
        if (xmlhttp.readyState==4 && xmlhttp.status==200)
        {
            if(req_type == 'menu_html'){
            var nav_text = xmlhttp.responseText.replaceAll('https://static.cloud.coveo.com/searchui/v2.9159/js/CoveoJsSearch.Lazy.min.js', '');
            nav_text = nav_text.replaceAll('src="/', 'src="'+maindomain_lang+'/');
            nav_text = nav_text.replaceAll("'/content", "'"+maindomain_lang+"/content");
                        
            document.getElementById("PAN_2021_NAV_ASYNC").innerHTML = nav_text.replaceAll('href="/', 'href="'+maindomain_lang+'/');

            var lozad_back = document.getElementsByClassName('lozad-background');
            Array.prototype.forEach.call(lozad_back, function(el) {
            // Do stuff here
            var el_back_img_path = el.getAttribute('data-background-image');
            var first_pos = el_back_img_path.indexOf("'");
            var last_pos = el_back_img_path.indexOf("'",first_pos+1);
            el_back_img_path = el_back_img_path.substring(first_pos+1,last_pos);
            el.setAttribute("data-background-image",main_site_url+el_back_img_path);
            });
            const observer_lozad = lozad('.lozad, .lozad-background'); // lazy loads elements with default selector as '.lozad'
            observer_lozad.observe();
            }
            if(req_type == 'head_inline_css'){
            addStyle(xmlhttp.responseText);
            }
        }
    }
    xmlhttp.open("GET", theUrl, true );
    xmlhttp.send();    
}    
    
if(referer == 'Prisma' || referer == 'CloudCortex' || referer == 'Cortex' || referer == 'Sase' || referer == 'Unit' || referer == 'Ngfw'){
    const article = document.querySelector('#PAN_2021_NAV_ASYNC');
    if(referer == 'Prisma'){
        article.dataset.type = 'prisma';
        $('#PAN_2021_NAV_ASYNC').removeClass('default').addClass('defaultRedesigned');
    }
    else if(referer == 'CloudCortex'){
        article.dataset.type = 'cloudcortex';
    }
    else if(referer == 'Cortex'){
        article.dataset.type = 'cortex';
    }
    else if(referer == 'Sase'){
        article.dataset.type = 'sase';
    }
    else if(referer == 'Unit'){
        article.dataset.type = 'unit';
    }
    else if(referer == 'Ngfw'){
        article.dataset.type = 'ngfw';
    }
    //set class to default
    if(referer == 'Unit' || referer == 'Ngfw'){
        $('#PAN_2021_NAV_ASYNC').removeClass('default').addClass('defaultRedesigned');
    }
    callMainSitePrismaNavHTML();
}
</script>

					<a href="//www.reddit.com/submit?url=https%3A%2F%2Funit42.paloaltonetworks.com%2Fimpersonation-campaigns-deliver-gh0st-rat%2F" target="_blank" role="link" title="Share in Reddit" data-page-track="true" data-page-track-value="impersonation-campaigns-deliver-gh0st-rat:share:reddit"> <img src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/themes/unit42-v6/dist/images/icons/icon-reddit-share.svg" alt="Reddit Icon"></a>

                <h2><a id="post-165582-_70d24ppf09fw"></a>Executive Summary</h2>
<p>We have identified two interconnected malware campaigns active throughout 2025, using large-scale brand impersonation to deliver Gh0st remote access Trojan (RAT) variants to Chinese-speaking users. From the first campaign to the second, the adversary advanced from simple droppers to complex, multi-stage infection chains that misuse legitimate, signed software to bypass modern defenses.</p>
<p>This report provides a detailed breakdown of the campaigns' anatomy, offering new intelligence on the attackers' operational playbook. We analyze an initial campaign from February–March 2025 that mimicked three brands across over 2,000 domains and a more sophisticated campaign starting in May 2025 that impersonated over 40 applications. The impersonated software primarily includes widely used enterprise tools, secure messaging apps, gaming platforms and popular AI software.</p>
<p>By analyzing the evolution of the attack methods, infrastructure and targeting, we establish a clear operational playbook. Understanding the adversary’s adaptive tactics, techniques and procedures (TTPs), such as using cloud infrastructure for payload delivery and DLL side-loading for evasion, provides crucial insights for enhancing security postures.</p>
<p>Our analysis is based on data from Palo Alto Networks products, including Advanced URL Filtering and Advanced WildFire, which provided visibility into the malware's behavior and infection chains. This internal data was supplemented by passive DNS (pDNS) analysis and open-source intelligence. We provide organizations with indicators of compromise (IoCs) to mitigate against this threat.</p>
<p>Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from this activity through the following products and services:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/wildfire" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced WildFire</a></li>
<li><a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/advanced-url-filtering/administration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced URL Filtering</a> and <a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/dns-security" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced DNS Security</a></li>
<li><a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/advanced-threat-prevention/administration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced Threat Prevention</a></li>
<li><a href="https://docs-cortex.paloaltonetworks.com/p/XDR" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cortex XDR</a> and <a href="https://docs-cortex.paloaltonetworks.com/p/XSIAM" target="_blank" rel="noopener">XSIAM</a></li>
<li>Cortex Cloud <a href="https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/cortex/cloud/data-security-posture-management" target="_blank" rel="noopener">DSPM</a></li>
</ul>
<p>If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the <a href="https://start.paloaltonetworks.com/contact-unit42.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Unit 42 Incident Response team</a>.</p>

<h2><a id="post-165582-_ekgryanfhzu1"></a>The Rise of Impersonation at Scale: A Persistent Threat to Chinese-Speaking Users</h2>
<p>In recent years, malware campaigns specifically tailored to target Chinese-speaking users globally have emerged as a notable trend in the threat landscape. These operations demonstrate a complex understanding of the target demographic's digital ecosystem and online behaviors.</p>
<p>The lures used are often not generic. Instead, attackers carefully select them to appeal to this specific audience. Attackers frequently impersonate the following types of applications:</p>
<ul>
<li>Software that is widely popular within the community (e.g., Youdao dictionary or Sogou browser)</li>
<li>Tools used to circumvent state-imposed internet restrictions (e.g., VPNs and encrypted messaging applications)</li>
</ul>
<p>How would potential victims find these malicious sites impersonating legitimate software? Attackers have a variety of options. They could generate traffic to these sites through malicious online ads or search engine poisoning. Attackers can also post on social media and other online forums to promote these sites. Email is another vector for leading potential victims to these sites.</p>
<p>The choice to target people seeking tools to bypass censorship is particularly strategic. This suggests an adversary who is interested in people already attempting to operate outside of easily monitored channels, making them prime targets for surveillance or espionage.</p>
<p>The final payload in these campaigns is often a RAT that grants the attacker comprehensive control over a compromised system. The Gh0st RAT and its many variants are a prominent choice, particularly for Chinese-nexus cybercrime and espionage actors who have used these tools for over a decade.</p>
<h2><a id="post-165582-_ypgnfnwb7vxa"></a>Anatomy of the First Campaign: Campaign Trio</h2>
<p>We refer to this initial activity as Campaign Trio due to its impersonation of three distinct software brands. Active from February–March 2025, this phase established a baseline operational model of the adversary. This campaign involved a massive number of domains, used an aggressive approach to infrastructure deployment and a clear, focused targeting strategy.</p>
<p>The malware distribution strategy of this campaign relied on a vast network of malicious websites that convincingly mimicked legitimate software download portals to lure victims.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_2f1j0rxf1aed"></a>Mass Domain Registration</h3>
<p>Between February and March 2025, attackers registered over 2,000 domains, with significant surges in activity in early February and early March. Attackers appear to have automated their domain registration, typically combining the impersonated brand name with a random-looking alphanumeric suffix and using TLDs like <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">.top</span> or <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">.vip</span>.</p>
<p>The entire network of over 2,000 domains was hosted on just three IP addresses:</p>
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">154.82.84[.]227</span></li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">156.251.25[.]43</span></li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">156.251.25[.]112</span></li>
</ul>
<p>This high-volume domain approach is designed to persist in the face of reputation-based blocking systems. It also ensures that even if some domains are taken down, many other domains remain available.</p>
<p>Figure 1 shows a sample attack infrastructure of Campaign Trio including the following info:</p>
<ul>
<li>Three clusters of brand impersonating domains</li>
<li>Their association with web server IP addresses</li>
<li>An additional server hosting the malware for downloading</li>
</ul>
<figure id="attachment_165594" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165594" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165594 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-367871-165582-1.png" alt="Diagram showing connections among malicious files, domains, and hosting IPs. Central cluster labeled &quot;Malicious files hosted on xiaxialiangwei dot com&quot; connects to three other clusters: one impersonating i4tools with various domain names, another showing hosting IPs like 156.251.85.113, and a third impersonating Youdao and DeepSeek with their respective domain names. Red lines indicate domain resolution, and dotted lines show file hosting connections." width="1000" height="668" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-367871-165582-1.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-367871-165582-1-659x440.png 659w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-367871-165582-1-1048x700.png 1048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-367871-165582-1-768x513.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-367871-165582-1-1536x1026.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165594" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1. The Campaign Trio's attack infrastructure.</figcaption></figure>
<p>This centralized model, with over 2,000 domains resolving to just three IP addresses, suggests that attackers viewed components of the infrastructure as disposable. This also implies an aggressive approach to infrastructure deployment that allows the attackers to rapidly establish new websites.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_vyhanualmtrz"></a>Targeted Impersonation</h3>
<p>The choice of impersonated brands for this campaign reveals a deliberate targeting strategy:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>i4tools:</strong> With over 1,400 domains, this was the most impersonated brand. This is Chinese-language, multi-function software for managing and transferring files to and from Apple-based mobile devices.</li>
<li><strong>Youdao:</strong> Attackers created over 600 domains to impersonate this popular Chinese dictionary and translation application, strongly indicating a focus on Chinese-speaking users.</li>
<li><strong>DeepSeek:</strong> We identified only five domains. The impersonation of this AI company demonstrates the attackers' interest in capitalizing on current technology trends.</li>
</ul>
<p>The landing pages hosted on these domains closely mimicked the legitimate sites to deceive victims into downloading the trojanized software installers shown in Figures 2, 3 and 4.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165605" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165605" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165605 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-372429-165582-2.png" alt="Screenshot of a fake DeepSeek V3 website homepage displayed on a computer screen, with the webpage source code visible at the bottom showing a specific line highlighted." width="1000" height="686" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-372429-165582-2.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-372429-165582-2-641x440.png 641w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-372429-165582-2-1020x700.png 1020w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-372429-165582-2-768x527.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-372429-165582-2-1536x1054.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165605" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2. Example of a malicious landing page impersonating DeepSeek and the malicious payload URL in the page content.</figcaption></figure>
<figure id="attachment_165616" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165616" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165616 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-377691-165582-3.png" alt="Screenshot of a web development environment showing code in a text editor on the right, and a browser window open to a fake version of the 'Youdao Dictionary' on the left, illustrating an example of web manipulation using JavaScript." width="1000" height="613" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-377691-165582-3.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-377691-165582-3-718x440.png 718w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-377691-165582-3-1142x700.png 1142w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-377691-165582-3-768x471.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-377691-165582-3-1536x941.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165616" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3. Example of a malicious landing page impersonating Youdao and the malicious payload URL in the page content.</figcaption></figure>
<figure id="attachment_165627" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165627" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165627 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-382874-165582-4.png" alt="Screenshot showing a web development environment with a view of a webpage in a browser that includes various graphical elements and text in Chinese characters. The lower part of the image displays the page's HTML source code highlighted, with annotations explaining specific parts of the code." width="1000" height="626" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-382874-165582-4.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-382874-165582-4-702x440.png 702w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-382874-165582-4-1117x700.png 1117w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-382874-165582-4-768x481.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-382874-165582-4-1536x962.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165627" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 4. Example of a malicious landing page impersonating i4tools and the malicious payload URL in the page content.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_6hznftbd5a2f"></a>Execution and Payload Delivery: A Centralized Model</h3>
<p>Webpages from over 2,000 domains served their malicious payloads from a single source: <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">hxxps[:]//xiazailianjieoss[.]com</span>.</p>
<p>This domain hosted ZIP archives containing the trojanized installers. The downloaded archives contained either a malicious Microsoft Installer (MSI) file or a standalone executable. The MSI installers used a custom action to execute a secondary, smaller executable, separating the malicious logic from the main installer to bypass static analysis.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_a52z9l5ainm2"></a>Final Payload: The Gh0st RAT</h3>
<p>MSI-based malware delivery can include a substantial variety of actions also typically executed by benign MSI files. This allows malicious actions to hide within the many legitimate operations generated by an attacker's MSI file.</p>
<p>Figure 5 illustrates this concept in action. It shows a malicious MSI sample from Campaign Trio running the embedded malware within the MSI package. Using Microsoft's <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/msi/orca-exe" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Orca</a> tool, we can search the malicious MSI file's custom actions for anything suspicious. Running the malicious executable is one of 43 custom actions, not including all the normal actions and processes generated by an MSI file.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165638" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165638" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165638 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-387598-165582-5.jpeg" alt="A screenshot showing an open installation window for a malicious file installation on a computer screen. Multiple windows are displayed including file explorer windows with folders and files related to a spoofed program, and dialog boxes detailing installation processes and settings. Arrows and annotations in red are present to guide the viewer through the installation steps. The interface is in a mix of English and Chinese language characters." width="1000" height="731" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-387598-165582-5.jpeg 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-387598-165582-5-602x440.jpeg 602w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-387598-165582-5-958x700.jpeg 958w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-387598-165582-5-768x561.jpeg 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-387598-165582-5-1536x1123.jpeg 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165638" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 5. Examining a malicious MSI file from Campaign Trio.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The MSI file in Figure 5 employs a seemingly legitimate graphical user interface (GUI) for its installation procedure. The Orca tool reveals the MSI file's custom action table, where we've highlighted the malicious action run in the background during the installation. In this instance, the custom action <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">LaunchApplication</span> executes the second-stage malware, a 1.7 MB executable named <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">[System Process]5.exe</span>.</p>
<p>Primary functions of <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">[System Process]5.exe</span> are to:</p>
<ul>
<li>Download an obfuscated binary from a staging server</li>
<li>Decode the binary and</li>
<li>Run it</li>
</ul>
<p>The obfuscated binary was hosted on URLs from <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">fs-im-kefu.7moor-fs1[.]com</span>, a malware distribution point <a href="https://dti.domaintools.com/chinese-malware-delivery-websites/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">linked to previous Gh0st RAT activity</a>.</p>
<p>The deobfuscated binary is the final payload. We identified this final payload as Gh0st RAT, which provides attackers with the following capabilities:</p>
<ul>
<li>Logging keystrokes</li>
<li>Capturing screenshots</li>
<li>Remote shell access</li>
<li>Downloading additional malware</li>
</ul>
<p>These Gh0st RAT samples create scheduled tasks for persistence and use <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">powershell.exe</span> to add exclusions in Windows Defender, so they can run undetected. Once active, these Gh0st RAT samples establish command and control (C2) communication via encrypted TCP traffic over port 8080 to servers with domains like <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">xiaobaituziha[.]com</span>, which resolved to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">103.181.134[.]138</span>.</p>
<h2><a id="post-165582-_7td6vxvd7j72"></a>Anatomy of the Second Campaign: Campaign Chorus</h2>
<p>We refer to the second campaign as Campaign Chorus because attackers expanded their lures to impersonate over 40 different software applications. Launched in May 2025, this campaign built upon the foundation of the first and showed a significant expansion in targeting. Its TTPs evolved to enhance evasion and bypass security controls.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_2uj1gaeyz20b"></a>Expanded Targeting, Refined TTPs</h3>
<p>While maintaining a focus on Chinese-speaking users, attackers broadened their lure selection to maximize their potential targets. The attackers organized Campaign Chorus in a more structured manner.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_om1gznrbqp4w"></a>Broader Scope and Wave-Based Attacks</h3>
<p>In Campaign Chorus, attackers impersonated widely used enterprise messaging software, Chinese versions of secure messaging apps and popular gaming platforms. They also continued targeting software popular with Chinese speakers, such as QQ Music and Sogou browser. This indicates a strategy to reach a wider demographic of Chinese speakers.</p>
<p>Figure 6 shows examples of impersonated applications from this campaign.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165649" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165649" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165649 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-390985-165582-6.png" alt="Four screenshots depicting spoofs of different app interfaces, including a music app, a software download page, a translation tool, and a version of the app in Chinese." width="1000" height="654" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-390985-165582-6.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-390985-165582-6-673x440.png 673w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-390985-165582-6-1071x700.png 1071w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-390985-165582-6-768x502.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-390985-165582-6-1536x1004.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165649" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 6. Software impersonation examples from The Chorus phase.</figcaption></figure>
<p>This campaign was initially executed in two distinct waves, distinguished by domain naming conventions and registration dates:</p>
<ul>
<li>Wave 1 (registered May 15, 2025): This wave consisted of 40 domains, all beginning with the prefix <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">guwaanzh</span></li>
<li>Wave 2 (registered May 26–28, 2025): This wave included 51 domains, all starting with the prefix <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">xiazaizhadia</span></li>
</ul>
<p>The use of structured, wave-based attacks with different domain prefixes and corresponding redirection servers (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">djbzdhygj[.]com</span> for Wave 1 and <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">yqmqhjgn[.]com</span> for Wave 2) suggests a more organized and possibly experimental approach. The attackers could have been testing the effectiveness of different lures or attempting to compartmentalize their infrastructure to make it more resilient to takedowns.</p>
<p>Figure 7 shows an infrastructure map diagram illustrating the two distinct attack waves, their respective redirection servers and how the domains were hosted on a single IP address.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165660" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165660" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165660 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-396469-165582-7.png" alt="Image depicting a Campaign Infrastructure Map, with two clusters of web entities interconnected. Each cluster contains multiple icons marked in red or pink, representing 'Single Attack Wave' and 'IP address Hosted in Hong Kong' respectively. Notable web entities such as yahoo.com and baidu.com are marked. The map includes a key for icon colors on the left lower corner and is labeled with various IP addresses." width="1000" height="504" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-396469-165582-7.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-396469-165582-7-786x396.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-396469-165582-7-1389x700.png 1389w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-396469-165582-7-768x387.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-396469-165582-7-1536x774.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165660" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 7. Attack infrastructure map for Campaign Chorus.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_8tkb5s35gjin"></a>A More Evasive Infection Chain</h3>
<p>Figure 8 shows the most significant advancement during this campaign: adopting a more intricate and elusive infection chain. This multi-stage evolution from the previous campaign increases the complexity of malware embedded in the MSI file. This indicates an increased effort to evade detection.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165671" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165671" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165671 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-401681-165582-8.png" alt="Diagram illustrating a cyber attack involving multiple components including a compromised system, malicious domain, redirection server, and a ZIP file. It shows the flow from infection to the delivery of a malicious VBscript with custom actions and finally to a compromised system." width="1000" height="481" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-401681-165582-8.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-401681-165582-8-786x378.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-401681-165582-8-1455x700.png 1455w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-401681-165582-8-768x369.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-401681-165582-8-1536x739.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165671" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 8. The multi-stage infection chain of Campaign Chorus.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The previous campaign's infection chain was more easily detectable by endpoint detection and response (EDR) solutions. This new chain is explicitly designed to circumvent these protections.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_mnkbwrc8g37h"></a>Redirection via Cloud-Hosted Payloads</h3>
<p>In a tactical shift from previous activity, the actor behind Campaign Chorus moved away from a single, self-hosted payload server. Instead, the malicious landing pages used intermediary redirection domains to fetch the malicious ZIP archives from public cloud service buckets.</p>
<p>For this tactic, attackers misused cloud services, leveraging trusted reputations to make malicious download traffic appear benign. Consequently, the malicious downloads might bypass network filters that would otherwise block traffic from an unknown or newly registered domain. This also increases the actor's operational resilience, as disrupting a cloud service bucket is a more involved process for defenders than simply blocklisting a malicious IP address.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_orf1pp5zxpp6"></a>The VBScript Dropper</h3>
<p>As noted earlier in Figure 8, the core of this new infection chain is an embedded VBScript file run as a custom action by the MSI installer. The VBScript file acts as a file assembler and decryptor for the next-stage malware.</p>
<p>This next-stage payload is stored within the MSI file, but not as a single encoded binary. Instead, it is split across multiple data files contained within the MSI's embedded <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">.cab</span> archive.</p>
<p>The VBScript file reads these separate components, merges them into a single binary and uses a stored password to decrypt the combined data. This process creates the next-stage malware. This technique is designed to evade static analysis tools that might otherwise detect a single binary containing malicious content within the MSI.</p>
<h3><a id="post-165582-_ue8ig546hokm"></a>Execution via DLL Side-Loading</h3>
<p>The final and most complex step in the infection chain is using DLL side-loading to execute the payload. The VBScript file decodes data binaries within the MSI and saves the resulting two files to disk:</p>
<ul>
<li>The first file is a copy of a legitimate signed executable (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">wsc_proxy.exe</span>)</li>
<li>The second file is a malicious, attacker-crafted DLL named <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">wsc.dll</span></li>
</ul>
<p>When <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">wsc_proxy.exe</span> is executed, the Windows loader searches for its required dependency, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">wsc.dll</span>. Because the malicious version is in the same directory, it is loaded into the process memory before the legitimate version in the system directory would be found.</p>
<p>This is a classic example of DLL side-loading. It is an <a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">evasion technique</a> that allows the attacker's code to run under the guise of a trusted, signed process. The misuse of a legitimate executable is meant to bypass application allow-listing and process-based monitoring. The parent process initiating the malicious activity is itself benign and digitally signed by a reputable vendor. This makes it significantly harder for security tools to flag the activity as malicious.</p>
<h2><a id="post-165582-_r7p0dqy7v0pj"></a>Campaign Profile: A Unified Operational Playbook</h2>
<p>When analyzed together, the evidence from Campaign Trio and Campaign Chorus reveals a consistent operational playbook, allowing us to build a distinct behavioral profile. The technical differences are best understood not as the work of two unrelated campaigns, but as the logical evolution of a single group adapting its methods.</p>
<p>The campaigns have several key characteristics that form a strategic signature:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Mass-scale programmatic infrastructure:</strong> Both campaigns rely on the programmatic generation of domains for brand impersonation using a consistent naming convention.</li>
<li><strong>Specific demographic focus:</strong> Both campaigns focus heavily on software popular with Chinese-speaking users, even when deploying infrastructure in regions like the U.S. and Singapore. This indicates an actor with a deep and specific understanding of this demographic, rather than an opportunistic actor casting a wide, generic net.</li>
<li><strong>“Burn-and-churn” operational tempo:</strong> Both campaigns use a centralized and disposable infrastructure model. The strategy of hosting thousands of domains on a handful of IP addresses demonstrates a rapid deployment approach where the attackers consider the infrastructure expendable. The actor prioritizes the speed and scale of deployment over stealth and long-term resilience. They are confident in their ability to quickly pivot to new domains and servers.</li>
<li><strong>Two-tiered infrastructure: </strong>The actor exhibits a clear separation between its disposable, high-volume access infrastructure (the thousands of impersonation domains) and its more critical operational infrastructure (the payload and C2 servers). While the access layer is designed to be burned, the operational layer shows evolution (from self-hosted to cloud-hosted) aimed at increasing longevity and resilience. This architectural choice allows the actor to absorb the loss of their frontend domains without losing its core payload delivery and C2 capabilities.</li>
</ul>

<h2><a id="post-165582-_kbbjr3hu1l6u"></a>Observed Campaign Activity and Infrastructure Expansion</h2>
<p>The results of our investigation reinforce that these campaigns are not isolated, short-term events. Attackers are actively maintaining and expanding their infrastructure, indicating a persistent, long-term operation.</p>
<p>We investigated the WHOIS creation dates of domains associated with both these campaigns and found that they have consistently registered domains from February–August 2025.</p>
<p>Our analysis showed a significant surge in activity between February and May 2025. During these four months, attackers created over 2,500 domains, accounting for 87.4% of all malicious domains identified in connection with these campaigns.</p>
<p>Figure 9 shows the distribution of domains belonging to these campaigns created per week according to their WHOIS creation dates. In February and March of 2025, we observed over 1,500 domains belonging to the first campaign being registered.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165682" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165682" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165682 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-405493-165582-9.png" alt="Bar chart showing the number of domains registered each week, with data from WHOIS. The x-axis represents weeks from February 2025 to 2025-07-28, and the y-axis shows the number of domains. Peak registration occurs in February and March. " width="1000" height="454" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-405493-165582-9.png 1157w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-405493-165582-9-786x357.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-405493-165582-9-768x348.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165682" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 9. Number of domains from the two campaigns registered over time, highlighting periodic bursts.</figcaption></figure>
<p>We also noticed an interesting pattern where attackers registered between 100-200 domains every week for a month.</p>
<ul>
<li>This pattern started with 100 domains registered on April 15, 2025.</li>
<li>This was followed by 237 more in the week of April 21–27, the majority of which were registered on April 22.</li>
<li>This was followed by another 191 domains between April 28–May 4. Of these, 104 were registered on April 29.</li>
<li>This was followed by a week of low activity (around May 5, 2025) and ended with almost 261 domains registered between May 13–15, 2025.</li>
</ul>
<p>This regularity suggests an automated or highly structured process for routine infrastructure replenishment, likely to replace domains that have been blocked during operations.</p>
<p>From the pDNS data, we find that these new domains are pointed to the same core IP addresses used in both campaigns, with activity observed as recently as July 2025. Domains associated with the first campaign's infrastructure continue to resolve to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">156.251.25[.]112</span>. For example, we observed domains such as <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">youdaxxyzr[.]top</span> and <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">i4toolscacsm[.]top</span> actively resolving to this IP address. This demonstrates that the actor did not simply abandon its initial core IP address infrastructure but continued to leverage the IP address for ongoing attacks.</p>
<p>Similarly, the infrastructure for the second campaign remains active. The IP address <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">95.173.197[.]195</span> continues to serve new malicious domains as of early October 2025. Continuously registering and refreshing domains is a clear tactic to evade blocklists. It helps ensure the longevity of the campaigns, pointing to a well-resourced and determined adversary.</p>
<p>Figure 10 depicts a graph of the first campaign. It shows domains involved in both campaigns. Both campaigns use the same elements, like nameservers and hosting IP addresses. This graph depicts 683 domains that share the same set of nameservers and resolve to the same hosting IP address <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">156.251.25[.]112</span>. This IP address is geolocated to Hong Kong.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165693" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165693" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165693 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-408318-165582-10.png" alt="Diagram showcasing the relationship between nameservers and IP addresses. On the left, a cluster of blue circles labeled 'nameserver record relation' and on the right, a cluster of orange circles labeled as 'resolving to the same IP address.' Arrows indicate relationships, with directional flow from blue to orange circles. Icons at the bottom indicate URL, file/sample, HK IP address, and domain in campaign, helping to explain elements in network activities." width="1000" height="739" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-408318-165582-10.png 1478w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-408318-165582-10-596x440.png 596w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-408318-165582-10-947x700.png 947w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-408318-165582-10-768x567.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165693" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 10. Large-scale activity graph showing infrastructure overlap between the two campaigns.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Furthermore, we analyzed pDNS query volumes for domains associated with the first campaign to quantify its sustained activity over time. We found that while daily query volumes fluctuated, there was a gradual upward trend in queries toward domains associated with this campaign between March 2025 and July 2025.</p>
<p>Figure 11 shows a large peak in the number of queries toward these domains on July 12, 2025. We investigated domains contributing to this peak, and over 68% of these queries were generated for domains registered between March 6 and March 13, 2025, representing an exact four-month gap. This increase in query volume could be due to changes in the attackers' content or their connections to other entities.</p>
<figure id="attachment_165704" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-165704" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-165704 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/chart.png" alt="Line graph showing historical traffic volume data with a significant spike on July 12, 2025." width="1000" height="454" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/chart.png 1157w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/chart-786x357.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/chart-768x348.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-165704" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 11. Normalized pDNS traffic volume of this activity from March through July 2025.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The parallel operation of both old and new infrastructure through sustained activity suggests an operation that is not merely evolving but consists of multiple infrastructures and distinct tool sets simultaneously. This could indicate A/B testing of TTPs, targeting different victim sets with different levels of complexity, or simply a cost-effective strategy of continuing to leverage older assets as long as they remain effective.</p>
<h2><a id="post-165582-_79xx36thuk4t"></a>Conclusion</h2>
<p>The campaigns detailed in this article represent a persistent, large-scale and evolving threat. Operating at scale combined with a continuous adaptation of TTPs presents a significant challenge for defenders.</p>
<p>There is a clear evolution in these two campaigns. Campaign Trio, the first campaign, uses direct droppers. Campaign Chorus, the second campaign, leverages a more complex multi-stage infection chain and uses DLL sideloading.</p>
<p>The following traits are notable:</p>
<ul>
<li>Consistent focus on a Chinese-speaking demographic</li>
<li>Programmatically generating thousands of domains</li>
<li>Strategically using both self-hosted and major cloud provider infrastructure</li>
</ul>
<p>This signals a broader trend where threat actors will increasingly leverage legitimate cloud services and signed software, shifting the defensive focus from blocking known-bad indicators to detecting sophisticated behavioral anomalies.</p>
<p>Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products:</p>
<ul>
